<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Lord Salisbury" and "The Marquess of Salisbury" redirect here. For other holders of the title, see <a href="/wiki/Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Marquess of Salisbury">Marquess of Salisbury</a>.</div>

<p><b>Robert Arthur Talbot Gascoyne-Cecil, 3rd Marquess of Salisbury</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Royal_Victorian_Order" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Royal Victorian Order">GCVO</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Deputy_Lieutenant" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy Lieutenant">DL</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="/ɔɪ/: &#39;oi&#39; in &#39;choice&#39;">ɔɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span></span><span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/əl/: &#39;le&#39; in &#39;bottle&#39;">əl</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> 3 February 1830&#160;&#8211;&#32;22 August 1903) was a British statesman and <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> three times for a total of over thirteen years. He was also <a href="/wiki/Foreign_Secretary" title="Foreign Secretary">Foreign Secretary</a> before and during most of his tenure. He avoided alignments or alliances, maintaining the policy of "<a href="/wiki/Splendid_isolation" title="Splendid isolation">splendid isolation</a>".
</p><p>Lord Robert Cecil, also known as Lord Salisbury, was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> in 1854 and served as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_India" title="Secretary of State for India">Secretary of State for India</a> in <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Lord Derby</a>'s Conservative government 1866–1867. In 1874, under <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Disraeli</a>, Salisbury returned as Secretary of State for India, and, in 1878, was appointed foreign secretary, and played a leading part in the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Berlin" title="Congress of Berlin">Congress of Berlin</a>. After Disraeli's death in 1881, Salisbury emerged as the Conservative leader in the House of Lords, with <a href="/wiki/Sir_Stafford_Northcote" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Stafford Northcote">Sir Stafford Northcote</a> leading the party in the Commons. He succeeded <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">William Ewart Gladstone</a> as prime minister in June 1885, and held the office until January 1886.
</p><p>When Gladstone came out in favour of <a href="/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule_movement" title="Irish Home Rule movement">Home Rule for Ireland</a>, Salisbury opposed him and formed an alliance with the breakaway <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Unionist_Party" title="Liberal Unionist Party">Liberal Unionists</a>, winning the <a href="/wiki/1886_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1886 United Kingdom general election">subsequent general election</a>. His biggest achievement in this term was obtaining the majority of the new territory in Africa during the <a href="/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa" title="Scramble for Africa">Scramble for Africa</a>, avoiding a war or serious confrontation with the other powers. He remained as prime minister until Gladstone's Liberals formed a government with the support of the <a href="/wiki/Irish_nationalists" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish nationalists">Irish nationalists</a> at the <a href="/wiki/1892_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1892 United Kingdom general election">1892 general election</a>. The Liberals, however, lost the <a href="/wiki/1895_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1895 United Kingdom general election">1895 general election</a>, and Salisbury for the third and last time became prime minister. He led Britain to victory in a <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">bitter, controversial war against the Boers</a>, and led the Unionists to another electoral victory in <a href="/wiki/1900_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1900 United Kingdom general election">1900</a>. He relinquished the premiership to his nephew <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a> in 1902 and died in 1903. He was the last prime minister to serve from the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historians agree that Salisbury was a strong and effective leader in foreign affairs, with a wide grasp of the issues. <a href="/wiki/Paul_Smith_(historian)" title="Paul Smith (historian)">Paul Smith</a> characterises his personality as "deeply neurotic, depressive, agitated, introverted, fearful of change and loss of control, and self-effacing but capable of extraordinary competitiveness."<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> A representative of the landed aristocracy, he held the <a href="/wiki/Reactionary" title="Reactionary">reactionary</a> credo, "Whatever happens will be for the worse, and therefore it is in our interest that as little should happen as possible."<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Searle says that instead of seeing his party's victory in 1886 as a harbinger of a new and more popular Conservatism, he longed to return to the stability of the past, when his party's main function was to restrain <a href="/wiki/Demagogic" class="mw-redirect" title="Demagogic">demagogic</a> liberalism and democratic excess.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He is generally <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom">ranked in the upper tier of British prime ministers</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life:_1830.E2.80.931852"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life:_1830–1852">Early life: 1830–1852</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life: 1830–1852">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Lord Robert Cecil was born at <a href="/wiki/Hatfield_House" title="Hatfield House">Hatfield House</a>, the third son of the <a href="/wiki/James_Gascoyne-Cecil,_2nd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="James Gascoyne-Cecil, 2nd Marquess of Salisbury">2nd Marquess of Salisbury</a> and Frances Mary, <i>née</i> Gascoyne. He was a patrilineal descendant of <a href="/wiki/William_Cecil,_1st_Baron_Burghley" title="William Cecil, 1st Baron Burghley">Lord Burghley</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Robert_Cecil,_1st_Earl_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Cecil, 1st Earl of Salisbury">1st Earl of Salisbury</a>, chief ministers of <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_I" title="Elizabeth I">Elizabeth I</a>. The family owned vast rural estates in <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire" title="Hertfordshire">Hertfordshire</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dorset" title="Dorset">Dorset</a>. This wealth increased sharply in 1821, when his father married his mother, Frances Mary Gascoyne, heiress of a wealthy merchant and Member of Parliament who had bought large estates in Essex and Lancashire.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 7">&#58;&#8202;7&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Robert had a miserable childhood, with few friends, and filled his time with reading. He was <a href="/wiki/Bullying" title="Bullying">bullied</a> unmercifully at the schools he attended.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 8–10">&#58;&#8202;8–10&#8202;</span></sup> In 1840, he went to <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a>, where he did well in French, German, Classics, and Theology, but left in 1845 because of intense bullying.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> His unhappy schooling shaped his pessimistic outlook on life and his negative views on democracy. He decided that most people were cowardly and cruel, and that the mob would run roughshod over sensitive individuals.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 10">&#58;&#8202;10&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In December 1847, he went to <a href="/wiki/Christ_Church,_Oxford" title="Christ Church, Oxford">Christ Church, Oxford</a>, where he received an honorary fourth class in Mathematics, conferred by nobleman's privilege due to ill health. Whilst at Oxford, he found the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_movement" class="mw-redirect" title="Oxford movement">Oxford movement</a> or "Tractarianism" to be an intoxicating force, and had an intense religious experience that shaped his life.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 12, 23">&#58;&#8202;12,&#8202;23&#8202;</span></sup> He was involved in the <a href="/wiki/Oxford_Union" title="Oxford Union">Oxford Union</a>, serving as its secretary and treasurer. In 1853, he was elected a prize fellow of <a href="/wiki/All_Souls_College,_Oxford" title="All Souls College, Oxford">All Souls College, Oxford</a>.
</p><p>In April 1850, he joined <a href="/wiki/Lincoln%27s_Inn" title="Lincoln&#39;s Inn">Lincoln's Inn</a>, but did not enjoy law.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15">&#58;&#8202;15&#8202;</span></sup> His doctor advised him to travel for his health, and so, from July 1851 to May 1853, Cecil travelled through <a href="/wiki/British_Cape_Colony" class="mw-redirect" title="British Cape Colony">Cape Colony</a>, Australia, including <a href="/wiki/Tasmania" title="Tasmania">Tasmania</a>, and New Zealand.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 15–16">&#58;&#8202;15–16&#8202;</span></sup> He disliked the Boers and wrote that free institutions and self-government could not be granted to the Cape Colony because the Boers outnumbered the British three-to-one, and "it will simply be delivering us over bound hand and foot into the power of the Dutch, who hate us as much as a conquered people can hate their conquerors".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 16">&#58;&#8202;16&#8202;</span></sup> He found the Native South Africans "a fine set of men – whose language bears traces of a very high former civilisation", similar to Italian. They were "an intellectual race, with great firmness and fixedness of will" but "horribly immoral" because they lacked theism.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 17">&#58;&#8202;17&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/Bendigo" title="Bendigo">Bendigo</a> goldfields in Australia, he claimed that "there is not half as much crime or insubordination as there would be in an English town of the same wealth and population". Ten thousand miners were policed by four men armed with carbines and, at <a href="/wiki/Mount_Alexander" title="Mount Alexander">Mount Alexander</a>, 30,000 people were protected by 200 policemen, with over 30,000 ounces (850,000&#160;g) of gold mined per week. He believed that there was "generally far more civility than I should be likely to find in the good town of <a href="/wiki/Hatfield,_Hertfordshire" title="Hatfield, Hertfordshire">Hatfield</a>" and claimed that was due to "the government was that of the Queen, not of the mob; from above, not from below. Holding from a supposed right (whether real or not, no matter)" and from "the People the source of all legitimate power,"<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 18">&#58;&#8202;18&#8202;</span></sup> Cecil said of the <a href="/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people" title="Māori people">Māori</a> of New Zealand: "The natives seem when they have converted to make much better Christians than the white man". A Maori chief offered Cecil 5 acres (2&#160;ha) near <a href="/wiki/Auckland" title="Auckland">Auckland</a>, which he declined.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 19">&#58;&#8202;19&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Member_of_Parliament:_1853.E2.80.931866"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Member_of_Parliament:_1853–1866">Member of Parliament: 1853–1866</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Member of Parliament: 1853–1866">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg/170px-Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg/255px-Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg/340px-Lord_Robert_Cecil_aetat_27.jpg 2x" data-file-width="382" data-file-height="507" /></a><figcaption>Cecil <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr>1857</figcaption></figure>
<p>Cecil entered the <a href="/wiki/British_House_of_Commons" class="mw-redirect" title="British House of Commons">House of Commons</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> on 22 August <a href="/wiki/Stamford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)#Elections_in_the_1850s" title="Stamford (UK Parliament constituency)">1853</a>, as <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">MP</a> for <a href="/wiki/Stamford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Stamford (UK Parliament constituency)">Stamford</a> in Lincolnshire. He retained this seat until he succeeded to his father's peerages in 1868 and it was not contested during his time as its representative. In his election address he opposed <a href="/wiki/Secular_education" title="Secular education">secular education</a> and "<a href="/wiki/Ultramontane" class="mw-redirect" title="Ultramontane">ultramontane</a>" interference with the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> which was "at variance with the fundamental principles of our constitution". He would oppose "any such tampering with our representative system as shall disturb the reciprocal powers on which the stability of our constitution rests".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 20">&#58;&#8202;20&#8202;</span></sup> In 1867, after his brother <a href="/wiki/Lord_Eustace_Cecil" title="Lord Eustace Cecil">Eustace</a> complained of being addressed by constituents in a hotel, Cecil responded: "A hotel infested by influential constituents is worse than one infested by bugs. It's a pity you can't carry around a powder insecticide to get rid of vermin of that kind".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 21">&#58;&#8202;21&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>In December 1856 Cecil began publishing articles for the <i><a href="/wiki/Saturday_Review_(London)" class="mw-redirect" title="Saturday Review (London)">Saturday Review</a></i>, to which he contributed anonymously for the next nine years. From 1861 to 1864 he published 422 articles in it; in total the weekly published 608 of his articles. The <i><a href="/wiki/Quarterly_Review" title="Quarterly Review">Quarterly Review</a></i> was the foremost intellectual journal of the age and of the twenty-six issues published between spring 1860 and summer 1866, Cecil had anonymous articles in all but three of them. He also wrote lead articles for the Tory daily newspaper the <i>Standard</i>. In 1859 Cecil was a founding co-editor of <i>Bentley's Quarterly Review</i>, with <a href="/wiki/John_Douglas_Cook" title="John Douglas Cook">John Douglas Cook</a> and Rev. <a href="/wiki/William_Scott_(Vicar_of_St_Olave%27s,_Jewry)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Scott (Vicar of St Olave&#39;s, Jewry)">William Scott</a>; but it closed after four issues.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 39–40">&#58;&#8202;39–40&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Salisbury criticised the foreign policy of <a href="/wiki/Lord_John_Russell" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord John Russell">Lord John Russell</a>, claiming he was "always being willing to sacrifice anything for peace... colleagues, principles, pledges... a portentous mixture of bounce and baseness... dauntless to the weak, timid and cringing to the strong". The lessons to be learnt from Russell's foreign policy, Salisbury believed, were that he should not listen to the opposition or the press otherwise "we are to be governed... by a set of weathercocks, delicately poised, warranted to indicate with unnerving accuracy every variation in public feeling". Secondly: "No one dreams of conducting national affairs with the principles which are prescribed to individuals. The meek and poor-spirited among nations are not to be blessed, and the common sense of Christendom has always prescribed for national policy principles diametrically opposed to those that are laid down in the <a href="/wiki/Sermon_on_the_Mount" title="Sermon on the Mount">Sermon on the Mount</a>". Thirdly: "The assemblies that meet in Westminster have no jurisdiction over the affairs of other nations. Neither they nor the Executive, except in plain defiance of international law, can interfere [in the internal affairs of other countries]... It is not a dignified position for a Great Power to occupy, to be pointed out as the busybody of Christendom". Finally, Britain should not threaten other countries unless prepared to back this up by force: "A willingness to fight is the <i><a href="/wiki/Point_d%27appui" title="Point d&#39;appui">point d'appui</a></i> of diplomacy, just as much as a readiness to go to court is the starting point of a lawyer's letter. It is merely courting dishonour, and inviting humiliation for the men of peace to use the habitual language of the men of war".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 40–42">&#58;&#8202;40–42&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_for_India:_1866.E2.80.931867"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_for_India:_1866–1867">Secretary of State for India: 1866–1867</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Secretary of State for India: 1866–1867">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1866 Lord Robert, now <a href="/wiki/Viscount_Cranborne" class="mw-redirect" title="Viscount Cranborne">Viscount Cranborne</a> after the death of his older brother, entered the third government of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Lord Derby</a> as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_India" title="Secretary of State for India">Secretary of State for India</a>. When in 1867 <a href="/wiki/John_Stuart_Mill" title="John Stuart Mill">John Stuart Mill</a> proposed a type of <a href="/wiki/Proportional_representation" title="Proportional representation">proportional representation</a>, Cranborne argued that: "It was not of our atmosphere—it was not in accordance with our habits; it did not belong to us. They all knew that it could not pass. Whether that was creditable to the House or not was a question into which he would not inquire; but every Member of the House the moment he saw the scheme upon the Paper saw that it belonged to the class of impracticable things".<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 2 August when the Commons debated the <a href="/wiki/Orissa_famine_of_1866" title="Orissa famine of 1866">Orissa famine</a> in India, Cranborne spoke out against experts, political economy, and the government of Bengal. Utilising the <a href="/wiki/Blue_book#United_Kingdom" title="Blue book">Blue Books</a>, Cranborne criticised officials for "walking in a dream... in superb unconsciousness, believing that what had been must be, and that as long as they did nothing absolutely wrong, and they did not displease their immediate superiors, they had fulfilled all the duties of their station". These officials worshipped political economy "as a sort of 'fetish'...&#160;[they] seemed to have forgotten utterly that human life was short, and that man did not subsist without food beyond a few days". Three-quarters of a million people had died because officials had chosen "to run the risk of losing the lives than to run the risk of wasting the money". Cranborne's speech was received with "an enthusiastic, hearty cheer from both sides of the House" and Mill crossed the floor of the Commons to congratulate him on it. The famine left Cranborne with a lifelong suspicion of experts and in the photograph albums at his home covering the years 1866–67 there are two images of skeletal Indian children amongst the family pictures.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 86">&#58;&#8202;86&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reform_Act_1867">Reform Act 1867</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Reform Act 1867">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1867" title="Reform Act 1867">Reform Act 1867</a></div>
<p>When parliamentary reform came to prominence again in the mid-1860s, Cranborne worked hard to master electoral statistics until he became an expert. When the Liberal Reform Bill was being debated in 1866, Cranborne studied the census returns to see how each clause in the Bill would affect the electoral prospects in each seat.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 86–87">&#58;&#8202;86–87&#8202;</span></sup> Cranborne did not expect Disraeli's conversion to reform, however. When the Cabinet met on 16 February 1867, Disraeli voiced his support for some extension of the suffrage, providing statistics amassed by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Dudley_Baxter" title="Robert Dudley Baxter">Robert Dudley Baxter</a>, showing that 330,000 people would be given the vote and all except 60,000 would be granted extra votes. Cranborne studied Baxter's statistics and on 21 February he met <a href="/wiki/Henry_Herbert,_4th_Earl_of_Carnarvon" title="Henry Herbert, 4th Earl of Carnarvon">Lord Carnarvon</a>, who wrote in his diary: "He is firmly convinced now that Disraeli has played us false, that he is attempting to hustle us into his measure, that Lord Derby is in his hands and that the present form which the question has now assumed has been long planned by him". They agreed to "a sort of offensive and defensive alliance on this question in the Cabinet" to "prevent the Cabinet adopting any very fatal course". Disraeli had "separate and confidential conversations...carried on with each member of the Cabinet from whom he anticipated opposition [which] had divided them and lulled their suspicions".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 89">&#58;&#8202;89&#8202;</span></sup> That same night Cranborne spent three hours studying Baxter's statistics and wrote to Carnarvon the day after that although Baxter was right overall in claiming that 30% of £10 ratepayers who qualified for the vote would not register, it would be untrue in relation to the smaller boroughs where the register is kept up to date. Cranborne also wrote to Derby arguing that he should adopt 10 shillings rather than Disraeli's 20 shillings for the qualification of the payers of direct taxation: "Now above 10 shillings you won't get in the large mass of the £20 householders. At 20 shillings I fear you won't get more than 150,000 double voters, instead of the 270,000 on which we counted. And I fear this will tell horribly on the small and middle-sized boroughs".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 90">&#58;&#8202;90&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg/220px-14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="270" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg/330px-14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg/440px-14th_Earl_of_Derby.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4060" data-file-height="4979" /></a><figcaption>Lord Derby. Salisbury resigned from his government in protest against proposals for parliamentary reform.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 23 February Cranborne protested in Cabinet and the next day analysed Baxter's figures using census returns and other statistics to determine how Disraeli's planned extension of the franchise would affect subsequent elections. Cranborne found that Baxter had not taken into account the different types of boroughs in the totals of new voters. In small boroughs under 20,000 the "fancy franchises" for direct taxpayers and dual voters would be less than the new working-class voters in each seat. The same day he met Carnarvon and they both studied the figures, coming to the same result each time: "A complete revolution would be effected in the boroughs" due to the new majority of the working-class electorate. Cranborne wanted to send his resignation to Derby along with the statistics but Cranborne agreed to Carnarvon's suggestion that as a Cabinet member he had a right to call a Cabinet meeting. It was planned for the next day, 25 February. Cranborne wrote to Derby that he had discovered that Disraeli's plan would "throw the small boroughs almost, and many of them entirely, into the hands of the voter whose qualification is less than £10. I do not think that such a proceeding is for the interest of the country. I am sure that it is not in accordance with the hopes which those of us who took an active part in resisting Mr Gladstone's Bill last year in those whom we induced to vote for us". The Conservative boroughs with populations less than 25,000 (a majority of the boroughs in Parliament) would be very much worse off under Disraeli's scheme than the Liberal Reform Bill of the previous year: "But if I assented to this scheme, now that I know what its effect will be, I could not look in the face those whom last year I urged to resist Mr Gladstone. I am convinced that it will, if passed, be the ruin of the Conservative party".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 90–92">&#58;&#8202;90–92&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>When Cranborne entered the Cabinet meeting on 25 February "with reams of paper in his hands" he began by reading statistics but was interrupted to be told of the proposal by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Stanley,_15th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Stanley, 15th Earl of Derby">Lord Stanley</a> that they should agree to a £6 borough rating franchise instead of the full household suffrage, and a £20 county franchise rather than £50. The Cabinet agreed to Stanley's proposal. The meeting was so contentious that a minister who was late initially thought they were debating the suspension of <i>habeas corpus</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 92–93">&#58;&#8202;92–93&#8202;</span></sup> The next day another Cabinet meeting took place, with Cranborne saying little and the Cabinet adopting Disraeli's proposal to bring in a Bill in a week's time. On 28 February a meeting of the <a href="/wiki/Carlton_Club" title="Carlton Club">Carlton Club</a> took place, with a majority of the 150 Conservative MPs present supporting Derby and Disraeli. At the Cabinet meeting on 2 March, Cranborne, Carnarvon and General Peel were pleaded with for two hours not to resign, but when Cranborne "announced his intention of resigning...Peel and Carnarvon, with evident reluctance, followed his example". <a href="/wiki/John_Manners,_7th_Duke_of_Rutland" title="John Manners, 7th Duke of Rutland">Lord John Manners</a> observed that Cranborne "remained unmoveable". Derby closed his red box with a sigh and stood up, saying "The Party is ruined!" Cranborne got up at the same time, with Peel remarking: "Lord Cranborne, do you hear what Lord Derby says?" Cranborne ignored this and the three resigning ministers left the room. Cranborne's resignation speech was met with loud cheers and Carnarvon observed that it was "moderate and in good taste – a sufficient justification for us who seceded and yet no disclosure of the frequent changes in policy in the Cabinet".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 93–95">&#58;&#8202;93–95&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Disraeli introduced his Bill on 18 March and it would extend the suffrage to all rate-paying householders of two years' residence, dual voting for graduates or those of a learned profession, or those with £50 in governments funds or in the Bank of England or a savings bank. These "fancy franchises", as Cranborne had foreseen, did not survive the Bill's course through Parliament; dual voting was dropped in March, the compound householder vote in April; and the residential qualification was reduced in May. In the end the county franchise was granted to householders rated at £12 annually.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 95">&#58;&#8202;95&#8202;</span></sup> On 15 July the third reading of the Bill took place and Cranborne spoke first, in a speech which his biographer Andrew Roberts has called "possibly the greatest oration of a career full of powerful parliamentary speeches".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 97">&#58;&#8202;97&#8202;</span></sup> Cranborne observed how the Bill "bristled with precautions, guarantees and securities" had been stripped of these. He attacked Disraeli by pointing out how he had campaigned against the Liberal Bill in 1866 yet the next year introduced a Bill more extensive than the one rejected. In the peroration Cranborne said:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I desire to protest, in the most earnest language which I am capable of using, against the political morality on which the manoeuvres of this year have been based. If you borrow your <a href="/wiki/Political_ethics" title="Political ethics">political ethics</a> from the ethics of the political adventurer, you may depend upon it the whole of your representative institutions will crumble beneath your feet. It is only because of that mutual trust in each other by which we ought to be animated, it is only because we believe that expressions and convictions expressed, and promises made, will be followed by deeds, that we are enabled to carry on this party Government which has led this country to so high a pitch of greatness. I entreat honourable Gentlemen opposite not to believe that my feelings on this subject are dictated simply by my hostility on this particular measure, though I object to it most strongly, as the House is aware. But, even if I took a contrary view – if I deemed it to be most advantageous, I still should deeply regret that the position of the Executive should have been so degraded as it has been in the present session: I should deeply regret to find that the House of Commons has applauded a policy of <i><a href="/wiki/Sleight_of_hand" title="Sleight of hand">legerdemain</a></i>; and I should, above all things, regret that this great gift to the people – if gift you think – should have been purchased at the cost of a political betrayal which has no parallel in our Parliamentary annals, which strikes at the root of all that mutual confidence which is the very soul of our party Government, and on which only the strength and freedom of our representative institutions can be sustained.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 98">&#58;&#8202;98&#8202;</span></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In his article for the October <i>Quarterly Review</i>, entitled 'The Conservative Surrender', Cranborne criticised Derby because he had "obtained the votes which placed him in office on the faith of opinions which, to keep office, he immediately repudiated...He made up his mind to desert these opinions at the very moment he was being raised to power as their champion". Also, the annals of modern parliamentary history could find no parallel for Disraeli's betrayal; historians would have to look "to the days when <a href="/wiki/Robert_Spencer,_2nd_Earl_of_Sunderland" title="Robert Spencer, 2nd Earl of Sunderland">Sunderland</a> directed the Council, and accepted the favours of <a href="/wiki/James_II_of_England" title="James II of England">James</a> when he was negotiating the invasion of <a href="/wiki/William_III_of_England" title="William III of England">William</a>". Disraeli responded in a speech that Cranborne was "a very clever man who has made a very great mistake".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 100">&#58;&#8202;100&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="In_opposition:_1868.E2.80.931874"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_opposition:_1868–1874">In opposition: 1868–1874</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: In opposition: 1868–1874">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1868, on the death of his father, he inherited the <a href="/wiki/Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Marquess of Salisbury">Marquessate of Salisbury</a>, thereby becoming a member of the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>. In 1869 he was elected <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_University_of_Oxford" class="mw-redirect" title="Chancellor of the University of Oxford">Chancellor of the University of Oxford</a> and elected a <a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">Fellow of the Royal Society</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Between 1868 and 1871, he was chairman of the <a href="/wiki/Great_Eastern_Railway" title="Great Eastern Railway">Great Eastern Railway</a>, which was then experiencing losses. During his tenure, the company was taken out of <a href="/wiki/Court_of_Chancery" title="Court of Chancery">Chancery</a>, and paid out a small dividend on its ordinary shares.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Cecil,_Vanity_Fair,_1869-07-10.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg/220px-Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="337" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg/330px-Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e6/Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg/440px-Robert_Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-07-10.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="918" /></a><figcaption>The Marquess of Salisbury caricatured by "<a href="/wiki/Carlo_Pellegrini_(caricaturist)" title="Carlo Pellegrini (caricaturist)">Ape</a>" (Carlo Pellegrini) in <i>Vanity Fair'</i>, 1869</figcaption></figure>
<p>From 1868 he was Honorary Colonel of the <a href="/wiki/Hertfordshire_Militia" title="Hertfordshire Militia">Hertfordshire Militia</a>, which became the <a href="/wiki/4th_(Militia)_Battalion,_Bedfordshire_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="4th (Militia) Battalion, Bedfordshire Regiment">4th (Militia) Battalion, Bedfordshire Regiment</a>, in 1881, and which was commanded in South Africa during the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a> by his eldest son.<sup id="cite_ref-kelly_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-kelly-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Secretary_of_State_for_India:_1874.E2.80.931878"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State_for_India:_1874–1878">Secretary of State for India: 1874–1878</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Secretary of State for India: 1874–1878">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Salisbury returned to government in 1874, serving once again as Secretary of State for India in the government of <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Benjamin Disraeli</a>, and Britain's Ambassador Plenipotentiary at the 1876 <a href="/wiki/Constantinople_Conference" title="Constantinople Conference">Constantinople Conference</a>. Salisbury gradually developed a good relationship with Disraeli, whom he had previously disliked and mistrusted.
</p><p>During a Cabinet meeting on 7 March 1878, a discussion arose over whether to occupy <a href="/wiki/Mytilene" title="Mytilene">Mytilene</a>. <a href="/wiki/Edward_Stanley,_15th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Stanley, 15th Earl of Derby">Lord Derby</a> recorded in his diary that "[o]f all present Salisbury by far the most eager for action: he talked of our sliding into a position of contempt: of our being humiliated etc."<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> At the Cabinet meeting the next day, Derby recorded that <a href="/wiki/John_Manners,_7th_Duke_of_Rutland" title="John Manners, 7th Duke of Rutland">Lord John Manners</a> objected to occupying the city "on the ground of right. Salisbury treated scruples of this kind with marked contempt, saying, truly enough, that if our ancestors had cared for the rights of other people, the British empire would not have been made. He was more vehement than any one for going on. In the end the project was dropped..."<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Foreign_Secretary:_1878.E2.80.931880"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary:_1878–1880">Foreign Secretary: 1878–1880</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary: 1878–1880">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1878, Salisbury became foreign secretary in time to help lead Britain to "peace with honour" at the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Berlin" title="Congress of Berlin">Congress of Berlin</a>. For this he was rewarded with the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter" title="Order of the Garter">Order of the Garter</a> along with Disraeli.
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition:_1881.E2.80.931885"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition:_1881–1885">Leader of the Opposition: 1881–1885</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition: 1881–1885">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Following Disraeli's death in 1881, the Conservatives entered a period of turmoil. The party's previous leaders had all been appointed as Prime Minister by the reigning monarch on advice from their retiring predecessor, and no process was in place to deal with leadership succession in case either the leadership became vacant while the party was in opposition, or the outgoing leader died without designating a successor, situations which both arose from the death of Disraeli (a formal leadership election system would not be adopted by the party until 1964, shortly after the government of <a href="/wiki/Alec_Douglas-Home" title="Alec Douglas-Home">Alec Douglas-Home</a> fell). Salisbury became the leader of the Conservative members of the House of Lords, though the overall leadership of the party was not formally allocated. So he struggled with the Commons leader <a href="/wiki/Stafford_Northcote,_1st_Earl_of_Iddesleigh" title="Stafford Northcote, 1st Earl of Iddesleigh">Sir Stafford Northcote</a>, a struggle in which Salisbury eventually emerged as the leading figure. Historian <a href="/wiki/Richard_Shannon_(historian)" title="Richard Shannon (historian)">Richard Shannon</a> argues that while Salisbury presided over one of the longest periods of Tory dominance, he misinterpreted and mishandled his election successes. Salisbury's blindness to the middle class and reliance on the aristocracy prevented the Conservatives from becoming a majority party.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/220px-Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="301" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/330px-Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/440px-Robert_Cecil_-_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg 2x" data-file-width="483" data-file-height="661" /></a><figcaption>Lord Salisbury.</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reform_Act_1884">Reform Act 1884</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Reform Act 1884">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1884" title="Representation of the People Act 1884">Representation of the People Act 1884</a></div>
<p>In 1884 Gladstone introduced a <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1884" title="Representation of the People Act 1884">Reform Bill</a> which would extend the suffrage to two million rural workers. Salisbury and Northcote agreed that any Reform Bill would be supported only if a parallel redistributionary measure was introduced as well. In a speech in the Lords, Salisbury claimed: "Now that the people have in no real sense been consulted, when they had, at the last General Election, no notion of what was coming upon them, I feel that we are bound, as guardians of their interests, to call upon the government to appeal to the people, and by the result of that appeal we will abide". The Lords rejected the Bill and Parliament was prorogued for ten weeks.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 295–6">&#58;&#8202;295–6&#8202;</span></sup> Writing to Canon <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_MacColl" title="Malcolm MacColl">Malcolm MacColl</a>, Salisbury believed that Gladstone's proposals for reform without redistribution would mean "the absolute effacement of the Conservative Party. It would not have reappeared as a political force for thirty years. This conviction...greatly simplified for me the computation of risks". At a meeting of the Carlton Club on 15 July, Salisbury announced his plan for making the government introduce a Seats (or Redistribution) Bill in the Commons whilst at the same time delaying a Franchise Bill in the Lords. The unspoken implication being that Salisbury would relinquish the party leadership if his plan was not supported. Although there was some dissent, Salisbury carried the party with him.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 297–8">&#58;&#8202;297–8&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Salisbury wrote to Lady John Manners on 14 June that he did not regard female suffrage as a question of high importance "but when I am told that my ploughmen are capable citizens, it seems to me ridiculous to say that educated women are not just as capable. A good deal of the political battle of the future will be a conflict between religion and unbelief: &amp; the women will in that controversy be on the right side".<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 21 July, a large meeting for reform was held at <a href="/wiki/Hyde_Park,_London" title="Hyde Park, London">Hyde Park</a>. Salisbury said in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Times" title="The Times">The Times</a></i> that "the employment of mobs as an instrument of public policy is likely to prove a sinister precedent". On 23 July at Sheffield, Salisbury said that the government "imagine that thirty thousand Radicals going to amuse themselves in London on a given day expresses the public opinion of the day...they appeal to the streets, they attempt legislation by picnic". Salisbury further claimed that Gladstone adopted reform as a "cry" to deflect attention from his foreign and economic policies at the next election. He claimed that the House of Lords was protecting the British constitution: "I do not care whether it is an hereditary chamber or any other – to see that the representative chamber does not alter the tenure of its own power so as to give a perpetual lease of that power to the party in predominance at the moment".
</p><p>On 25 July at a reform meeting in Leicester consisting of 40,000 people, Salisbury was burnt in effigy and a banner quoted Shakespeare's <i>Henry VI</i>: "Old Salisbury – shame to thy silver hair, Thou mad misleader". On 9 August in Manchester, over 100,000 came to hear Salisbury speak. On 30 September at Glasgow, he said: "We wish that the franchise should pass but that before you make new voters you should determine the constitution in which they are to vote".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 298–300">&#58;&#8202;298–300&#8202;</span></sup> Salisbury published an article in the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Review_(London)" title="National Review (London)">National Review</a></i> for October, titled 'The Value of Redistribution: A Note on Electoral Statistics'. He claimed that the Conservatives "have no cause, for Party reasons, to dread enfranchisement coupled with a fair redistribution". Judging by the 1880 results, Salisbury asserted that the overall loss to the Conservatives of enfranchisement without redistribution would be 47 seats. Salisbury spoke throughout Scotland and claimed that the government had no mandate for reform when it had not appealed to the people.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 300–1">&#58;&#8202;300–1&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Gladstone offered wavering Conservatives a compromise a little short of enfranchisement and redistribution, and after the Queen unsuccessfully attempted to persuade Salisbury to compromise, he wrote to Rev. James Baker on 30 October: "Politics stand alone among human pursuits in this characteristic, that no one is conscious of liking them – and no one is able to leave them. But whatever affection they may have had they are rapidly losing. The difference between now and thirty years ago when I entered the House of Commons is inconceivable".
</p><p>On 11 November, the Franchise Bill received its third reading in the Commons and it was due to get a second reading in the Lords. The day after at a meeting of Conservative leaders, Salisbury was outnumbered in his opposition to compromise. On 13 February, Salisbury rejected MacColl's idea that he should meet Gladstone, as he believed the meeting would be found out and that Gladstone had no genuine desire to negotiate. On 17 November, it was reported in the newspapers that if the Conservatives gave "adequate assurance" that the Franchise Bill would pass the Lords before Christmas the government would ensure that a parallel Seats Bill would receive its second reading in the Commons as the Franchise Bill went into committee stage in the Lords. Salisbury responded by agreeing only if the Franchise Bill came second.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 303–4">&#58;&#8202;303–4&#8202;</span></sup> The Carlton Club met to discuss the situation, with Salisbury's daughter writing:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>The three arch-funkers <a href="/wiki/Hugh_Cairns,_1st_Earl_Cairns" title="Hugh Cairns, 1st Earl Cairns">Cairns</a>, <a href="/wiki/Charles_Gordon-Lennox,_6th_Duke_of_Richmond" title="Charles Gordon-Lennox, 6th Duke of Richmond">Richmond</a> and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Herbert,_4th_Earl_of_Carnarvon" title="Henry Herbert, 4th Earl of Carnarvon">Carnarvon</a> cried out declaring that he would accept no compromise at all as it was absurd to imagine the Government conceding it. When the discussion was at its height (very high) enter <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur</a> [Balfour] with explicit declamation dictated by <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">GOM</a> in <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Cavendish,_8th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="Spencer Cavendish, 8th Duke of Devonshire">Hartington's</a> handwriting yielding the point entirely. Tableau and triumph along the line for the 'stiff' policy which had obtained terms which the funkers had not dared hope for. My father's prevailing sentiment is one of complete wonder...we have got all and more than we demanded.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 305">&#58;&#8202;305&#8202;</span></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Despite the controversy which had raged, the meetings of leading Liberals and Conservatives on reform at <a href="/wiki/Downing_Street" title="Downing Street">Downing Street</a> were amicable. Salisbury and the Liberal <a href="/wiki/Sir_Charles_Dilke,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir Charles Dilke, 2nd Baronet">Sir Charles Dilke</a> dominated discussions as they had both closely studied in detail the effects of reform on the constituencies. After one of the last meetings on 26 November, Gladstone told his secretary that "Lord Salisbury, who seems to monopolise all the say on his side, has no respect for tradition. As compared with him, Mr Gladstone declares he is himself quite a Conservative. They got rid of the boundary question, minority representation, grouping and the Irish difficulty. The question was reduced to... for or against single member constituencies". The <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1884" title="Representation of the People Act 1884">Reform Bill</a> laid down that the majority of the 670 constituencies were to be roughly equal size and return one member; those between 50,000 and 165,000 kept the two-member representation and those over 165,000 and all the counties were split up into single-member constituencies. This franchise existed until <a href="/wiki/Representation_of_the_People_Act_1918" title="Representation of the People Act 1918">1918</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 305–6">&#58;&#8202;305–6&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister:_1885-1892">Prime minister: 1885-1892<span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Prime minister: 1885-1892">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_term:_1885-1886">First term: 1885-1886</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: First term: 1885-1886">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Salisbury_ministry" title="First Salisbury ministry">First Salisbury ministry</a></div>
<p>Salisbury became prime minister of a minority administration from 1885 to 1886.
In the November 1883 issue of <i>National Review</i> Salisbury wrote an article titled "Labourers' and Artisans' Dwellings" in which he argued that the poor conditions of working class housing were injurious to morality and health.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 282">&#58;&#8202;282&#8202;</span></sup> Salisbury said "<i><a href="/wiki/Laissez-faire" title="Laissez-faire">Laissez-faire</a></i> is an admirable doctrine but it must be applied on both sides", as Parliament had enacted new building projects (such as the <a href="/wiki/Thames_Embankment" title="Thames Embankment">Thames Embankment</a>) which had displaced working-class people and was responsible for "packing the people tighter": "...thousands of families have only a single room to dwell in, where they sleep and eat, multiply, and die... It is difficult to exaggerate the misery which such conditions of life must cause, or the impulse they must give to vice. The depression of body and mind which they create is an almost insuperable obstacle to the action of any elevating or refining agencies".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 283">&#58;&#8202;283&#8202;</span></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Pall_Mall_Gazette" class="mw-redirect" title="Pall Mall Gazette">Pall Mall Gazette</a></i> argued that Salisbury had sailed into "the turbid waters of State Socialism"; the <i><a href="/wiki/Manchester_Guardian" class="mw-redirect" title="Manchester Guardian">Manchester Guardian</a></i> said his article was "State socialism pure and simple" and <i>The Times</i> claimed Salisbury was "in favour of state socialism".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 283–4">&#58;&#8202;283–4&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_reforms_and_parliamentary_majority">Early reforms and parliamentary majority</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Early reforms and parliamentary majority">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In July 1885 the <a href="/wiki/Housing_of_the_Working_Classes_Act_1885" title="Housing of the Working Classes Act 1885">Housing of the Working Classes Bill</a> was introduced by the Home Secretary, <a href="/wiki/R._A._Cross,_1st_Viscount_Cross" title="R. A. Cross, 1st Viscount Cross">R. A. Cross</a> in the Commons and Salisbury in the Lords. When <a href="/wiki/Francis_Charteris,_10th_Earl_of_Wemyss" title="Francis Charteris, 10th Earl of Wemyss">Lord Wemyss</a> criticised the Bill as "strangling the spirit of independence and the self-reliance of the people, and destroying the moral fibre of our race in the anaconda coils of state socialism", Salisbury responded: "Do not imagine that by merely affixing to it the reproach of Socialism you can seriously affect the progress of any great legislative movement, or destroy those high arguments which are derived from the noblest principles of philanthropy and religion". The Bill ultimately passed and came into effect on 14 August 1885.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 286">&#58;&#8202;286&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Although unable to accomplish much due to his lack of a parliamentary majority, the split of the Liberals over <a href="/wiki/Irish_Home_Rule_movement" title="Irish Home Rule movement">Irish Home Rule</a> in 1886 enabled him to return to power with a majority, and, excepting a Liberal minority government (1892–95), to serve as prime minister from 1886 to 1902.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_term:_1886-1892">Second term: 1886-1892<span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Second term: 1886-1892">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_Salisbury_ministry" title="Second Salisbury ministry">Second Salisbury ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil,_Vanity_Fair,_1900-12-20.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg/220px-Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="363" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg/330px-Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8b/Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg/440px-Robert_Arthur_Talbot_Gascoyne-Cecil%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1900-12-20.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3813" data-file-height="6300" /></a><figcaption>Salisbury caricatured by <a href="/wiki/Leslie_Ward" title="Leslie Ward">Spy</a> for <a href="/wiki/Vanity_Fair_(British_magazine)" title="Vanity Fair (British magazine)">Vanity Fair</a>, 1900</figcaption></figure>
<p>Salisbury was back in office, although without a conservative majority; he depended on the Liberal Unionists, led by <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Cavendish,_8th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="Spencer Cavendish, 8th Duke of Devonshire">Lord Hartington</a>. Maintaining the alliance forced Salisbury to make concessions in support of progressive legislation regarding Irish land purchases, education, and county councils. His nephew <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a> acquired a strong reputation for resolute coercion in Ireland, and was promoted to leadership in the Commons in 1891. The Prime Minister proved adept at his handling of the press, as Sir <a href="/wiki/Edward_Walter_Hamilton" title="Edward Walter Hamilton">Edward Walter Hamilton</a> noted in his diary in 1887 he was: "the prime minister most accessible to the press. He is not prone to give information: but when he does, he gives it freely, &amp; his information can always be relied on."<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Salisbury once again kept the foreign office (from January 1887), and his diplomacy continued to display a high level of skill, avoiding the extremes of Gladstone on the left and Disraeli on the right. His policy rejected entangling alliances–which at the time and ever since has been called "<a href="/wiki/Splendid_isolation" title="Splendid isolation">splendid isolation</a>". He was successful in negotiating differences over colonial claims with France and others.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>  The major problems were in the Mediterranean, where British interest had been involved for a century. It was now especially important to protect the <a href="/wiki/Suez_Canal" title="Suez Canal">Suez Canal</a> and the sea lanes to India and Asia. He ended Britain's isolation through the <a href="/wiki/Mediterranean_Agreements_(1887)" title="Mediterranean Agreements (1887)">Mediterranean Agreements</a> (March and December 1887) with <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy" title="Kingdom of Italy">Italy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Austria-Hungary" title="Austria-Hungary">Austria-Hungary</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He saw the need for maintaining control of the seas and passed the <a href="/wiki/Naval_Defence_Act_1889" title="Naval Defence Act 1889">Naval Defence Act 1889</a>, which facilitated the spending of an extra £20&#160;million on the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> over the following four years. This was the biggest ever expansion of the navy in peacetime: ten new <a href="/wiki/Battleship" title="Battleship">battleships</a>, thirty-eight new <a href="/wiki/Cruiser" title="Cruiser">cruisers</a>, eighteen new <a href="/wiki/Torpedo_boat" title="Torpedo boat">torpedo boats</a> and four new fast <a href="/wiki/Gunboat" title="Gunboat">gunboats</a>. Traditionally (since the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Trafalgar" title="Battle of Trafalgar">Battle of Trafalgar</a>) Britain had possessed a navy one-third larger than their nearest naval rival but now the Royal Navy was set to the <a href="/wiki/Two-power_standard" class="mw-redirect" title="Two-power standard">two-power standard</a>; that it would be maintained "to a standard of strength equivalent to that of the combined forces of the next two biggest navies in the world".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 540">&#58;&#8202;540&#8202;</span></sup> This was aimed at <a href="/wiki/French_Third_Republic" title="French Third Republic">France</a> and <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>.
</p><p>Salisbury was offered a <a href="/wiki/Duke" title="Duke">dukedom</a> by <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> in 1886 and 1892, but declined both offers, citing the prohibitive cost of the lifestyle dukes were expected to maintain and stating that he would rather have an ancient <a href="/wiki/Marquessate" class="mw-redirect" title="Marquessate">marquessate</a> than a modern dukedom.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 374–5">&#58;&#8202;374–5&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="1890_Ultimatum_on_Portugal">1890 Ultimatum on Portugal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: 1890 Ultimatum on Portugal">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1890_British_Ultimatum" title="1890 British Ultimatum">1890 British Ultimatum</a></div>
<p>Trouble arose with Portugal, which had overextended itself in building a colonial empire in Africa it could ill afford. There was a clash of colonial visions between <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal" title="Kingdom of Portugal">Portugal</a> (the "<a href="/wiki/Pink_Map" title="Pink Map">Pink Map</a>", produced by the <a href="/wiki/Lisbon_Geographic_Society" title="Lisbon Geographic Society">Lisbon Geographic Society</a> after <a href="/wiki/Alexandre_de_Serpa_Pinto" title="Alexandre de Serpa Pinto">Alexandre de Serpa Pinto</a>'s, <a href="/wiki/Hermenegildo_Capelo" title="Hermenegildo Capelo">Hermenegildo Capelo</a>'s and <a href="/wiki/Roberto_Ivens" title="Roberto Ivens">Roberto Ivens</a>'s expeditions to Africa) and the British Empire  (<a href="/wiki/Cecil_Rhodes" title="Cecil Rhodes">Cecil Rhodes</a>'s "<a href="/wiki/Cape_to_Cairo_Railway" title="Cape to Cairo Railway">Cape to Cairo Railway</a>") came after years of diplomatic conflict about several African territories with Portugal and other powers. Portugal, financially hard-pressed, had to abandon several territories corresponding to today's <a href="/wiki/Malawi" title="Malawi">Malawi</a>, <a href="/wiki/Zambia" title="Zambia">Zambia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a> in favour of the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Controversies">Controversies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Controversies">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1889 Salisbury set up the <a href="/wiki/London_County_Council" title="London County Council">London County Council</a> and then in 1890 allowed it to build houses. However, he came to regret this, saying in November 1894 that the LCC, "is the place where collectivist and socialistic experiments are tried. It is the place where a new revolutionary spirit finds its instruments and collects its arms".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 501">&#58;&#8202;501&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Salisbury caused controversy in 1888 after <a href="/wiki/Gainsford_Bruce" title="Gainsford Bruce">Gainsford Bruce</a> had won the <a href="/wiki/Holborn_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Holborn (UK Parliament constituency)">Holborn</a> by-election for the Unionists, beating the Liberal <a href="/wiki/William_Compton,_5th_Marquess_of_Northampton" title="William Compton, 5th Marquess of Northampton">Lord Compton</a>. Bruce had won the seat with a smaller majority than <a href="/wiki/Francis_Duncan" title="Francis Duncan">Francis Duncan</a> had for the Unionists in 1885. Salisbury explained this by saying in a speech in Edinburgh on 30 November: "But then Colonel Duncan was opposed to a black man, and, however great the progress of mankind has been, and however far we have advanced in overcoming prejudices, I doubt if we have yet got to the point where a British constituency will elect a black man to represent them.... I am speaking roughly and using language in its colloquial sense, because I imagine the colour is not exactly black, but at all events, he was a man of another race".
</p><p>The "black man" was <a href="/wiki/Dadabhai_Naoroji" title="Dadabhai Naoroji">Dadabhai Naoroji</a>, an Indian <a href="/wiki/Parsi" class="mw-redirect" title="Parsi">Parsi</a>. Salisbury's comments were criticised by the Queen and by Liberals who believed that Salisbury had suggested that only white Britons could represent a British constituency. Three weeks later, Salisbury delivered a speech at Scarborough, where he denied that "the word "black" necessarily implies any contemptuous denunciation: "Such a doctrine seems to be a scathing insult to a very large proportion of the human race... The people whom we have been fighting at <a href="/wiki/Suakin" title="Suakin">Suakin</a>, and whom we have happily conquered, are among the finest tribes in the world, and many of them are as black as my hat". Furthermore, "such candidatures are incongruous and unwise. The British House of Commons, with its traditions... is a machine too peculiar and too delicate to be managed by any but those who have been born within these isles". Naoroji was elected for <a href="/wiki/Finsbury_Central_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Finsbury Central (UK Parliament constituency)">Finsbury</a> in 1892 and Salisbury invited him to become a Governor of the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_Institute" class="mw-redirect" title="Commonwealth Institute">Imperial Institute</a>, which he accepted.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 506">&#58;&#8202;506&#8202;</span></sup> In 1888, the <a href="/wiki/New_York_Times" class="mw-redirect" title="New York Times">New York Times</a> published an article that was extremely critical of Lord Salisbury's remark. It included the following quotation, "Of course the parsees are not black men, but the purest Aryan type in existence, with an average complexion fairer than Lord Salisbury's; but even if they were ebony hued it would be grotesque and foolish for a Prime Minister of England&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; to insult them in such a wanton fashion as this."<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Documents in the Foreign Office archives revealed that Salisbury was made aware of a rape in 1891 and other atrocities carried out against women and children in the <a href="/wiki/Niger_Delta" title="Niger Delta">Niger Delta</a> by Consul George Annesley and his soldiers but took no action against Annesley, who was "quietly pensioned off."<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Leader_of_the_Opposition:_1892.E2.80.931895"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Opposition:_1892–1895">Leader of the Opposition: 1892–1895</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Leader of the Opposition: 1892–1895">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In the aftermath of the <a href="/wiki/1892_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1892 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1892</a>, Balfour and Chamberlain wished to pursue a programme of social reform, which Salisbury believed would alienate "a good many people who have always been with us" and that "these social questions are destined to break up our party".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> When the Liberals and Irish Nationalists (which were a majority in the new Parliament) successfully voted against the government, Salisbury resigned the premiership on 12 August. His private secretary at the Foreign Office wrote that Salisbury "shewed indecent joy at his release".<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Salisbury—in an article in November for the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Review_(London)" title="National Review (London)">National Review</a></i> entitled 'Constitutional revision'—said that the new government, lacking a majority in England and Scotland, had no mandate for Home Rule and argued that because there was no referendum only the House of Lords could provide the necessary consultation with the nation on policies for organic change.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The Lords defeated the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Ireland_Bill_1893" title="Government of Ireland Bill 1893">second Home Rule Bill</a> by 419 to 41 in September 1893, but Salisbury stopped them from opposing the Liberal Chancellor's death duties in 1894.
