In 1894 Salisbury also became president of the <a href="/wiki/British_Science_Association" title="British Science Association">British Association for the Advancement of Science</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-SmutsPapers1918-19_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-SmutsPapers1918-19-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> presenting a notable inaugural address on 4 August of that year.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/1895_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1895 United Kingdom general election">general election of 1895</a> returned a large Unionist majority.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_minister:_1895.E2.80.931902"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_minister:_1895–1902">Prime minister: 1895–1902<span class="anchor" id="Third_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Prime minister: 1895–1902">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Unionist_government,_1895%E2%80%931905" title="Unionist government, 1895–1905">Unionist government, 1895–1905</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:British_Empire_in_1898.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/British_Empire_in_1898.png/330px-British_Empire_in_1898.png" decoding="async" width="330" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/British_Empire_in_1898.png/495px-British_Empire_in_1898.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/British_Empire_in_1898.png/660px-British_Empire_in_1898.png 2x" data-file-width="1434" data-file-height="624" /></a><figcaption>The British Empire in 1898</figcaption></figure>
<p>Salisbury's expertise was in foreign affairs. For most of his time as prime minister he served not as <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury" title="First Lord of the Treasury">First Lord of the Treasury</a>, the traditional position held by the prime minister, but as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_and_Commonwealth_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign and Commonwealth Affairs">foreign secretary</a>. In that capacity, he managed Britain's foreign affairs, but he was being sarcastic about a policy of "<a href="/wiki/Splendid_isolation" title="Splendid isolation">Splendid isolation</a>"—such was not his goal.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy_2">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Timeline_of_British_diplomatic_history#1897–1919" title="Timeline of British diplomatic history">Timeline of British diplomatic history §&#160;1897–1919</a></div>
<p>In the foreign affairs Salisbury was challenged worldwide, The long-standing policy of "<a href="/wiki/Splendid_isolation" title="Splendid isolation">Splendid isolation</a>" had left Britain with no allies and few friends. In Europe, Germany was worrisome regarding its growing industrial and naval power, <a href="/wiki/Wilhelm_II_of_Germany" class="mw-redirect" title="Wilhelm II of Germany">Kaiser Wilhelm</a>'s erratic foreign policy, and the instability caused by the decline of the Ottoman Empire. France was threatening British control of Sudan. In the Americas, for domestic political reasons, U.S. <a href="/wiki/Presidencies_of_Grover_Cleveland" title="Presidencies of Grover Cleveland">President Cleveland</a> manufactured a quarrel over <a href="/wiki/Venezuela" title="Venezuela">Venezuela</a>'s border with <a href="/wiki/British_Guiana" title="British Guiana">British Guiana</a>. In South Africa conflict was threatening with the two Boer republics. In the <a href="/wiki/The_Great_Game" class="mw-redirect" title="The Great Game">Great Game</a> in Central Asia, the line that separated Russia and British India in 1800 was narrowing.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  In China the British economic dominance was threatened by other powers that wanted to control slices of China.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Twist-British-Tail.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Twist-British-Tail.jpg/310px-Twist-British-Tail.jpg" decoding="async" width="310" height="205" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Twist-British-Tail.jpg/465px-Twist-British-Tail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Twist-British-Tail.jpg/620px-Twist-British-Tail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="846" data-file-height="559" /></a><figcaption>President Cleveland twists the tail of the British Lion regarding Venezuela—a policy hailed by Irish Catholics in the United States; cartoon in <i>Puck</i> by J.S. Pughe, 1895</figcaption></figure>
<p>The tension with Germany had subsided in 1890 after <a href="/wiki/Heligoland%E2%80%93Zanzibar_Treaty" title="Heligoland–Zanzibar Treaty">a deal</a> exchanged German holdings in East Africa for <a href="/wiki/Heligoland" title="Heligoland">an island</a> off the German coast. However, with peace-minded Bismarck retired by an aggressive new Kaiser, tensions rose and negotiations faltered.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>  France retreated in Africa after the British dominated in the <a href="/wiki/Fashoda_Incident" title="Fashoda Incident">Fashoda Incident</a>. The Venezuela crisis was settled amicably and London and Washington became friendly after Salisbury gave Washington what it wanted in the <a href="/wiki/Alaska_boundary_dispute" title="Alaska boundary dispute">Alaska boundary dispute</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Open_Door_Policy" title="Open Door Policy">Open Door Policy</a> and a 1902 treaty with Japan resolved the China crisis. However, in South Africa a nasty <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Boer war</a> broke out in 1899 and for a few months it seemed the Boers were winning.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Venezuela_crisis_with_the_United_States">Venezuela crisis with the United States</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Venezuela crisis with the United States">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1895 the <a href="/wiki/Venezuelan_crisis_of_1895" title="Venezuelan crisis of 1895">Venezuelan crisis with the United States erupted</a>. A border dispute between the colony of <a href="/wiki/British_Guiana" title="British Guiana">British Guiana</a> and <a href="/wiki/United_States_of_Venezuela" title="United States of Venezuela">Venezuela</a> caused a major Anglo-American crisis when the United States intervened to take Venezuela's side. Propaganda sponsored by Venezuela convinced American public opinion that the British were infringing on Venezuelan territory. The United States demanded an explanation and Salisbury refused. The crisis escalated when President <a href="/wiki/Grover_Cleveland" title="Grover Cleveland">Grover Cleveland</a>, citing the <a href="/wiki/Monroe_Doctrine" title="Monroe Doctrine">Monroe Doctrine</a>, issued an ultimatum in late 1895. Salisbury's cabinet convinced him he had to go to arbitration. Both sides calmed down and the issue was quickly resolved through arbitration which largely upheld the British position on the legal boundary line. Salisbury remained angry but a consensus was reached in London, led by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Petty-Fitzmaurice,_5th_Marquess_of_Lansdowne" title="Henry Petty-Fitzmaurice, 5th Marquess of Lansdowne">Lord Landsdowne</a>, to seek much friendlier relations with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> By standing with a Latin American nation against the encroachment of the British, the US improved relations with the Latin Americans, and the cordial manner of the procedure improved American diplomatic relations with Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Despite the popularity of the Boers in American public opinion, official Washington supported London in the Second Boer War.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Africa">Africa</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Africa">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>An Anglo-German agreement (1890) resolved conflicting claims in East Africa; Great Britain received large territories in Zanzibar and Uganda in exchange for the small island of <a href="/wiki/Helgoland" class="mw-redirect" title="Helgoland">Helgoland</a> in the North Sea. Negotiations with Germany on broader issues failed. In January 1896 the reckless German Kaiser Wilhelm II escalated tensions in South Africa with his <a href="/wiki/Kruger_telegram" title="Kruger telegram">Kruger telegram</a> congratulating Boer President <a href="/wiki/Paul_Kruger" title="Paul Kruger">Paul Kruger</a> of the Transvaal for beating off the British <a href="/wiki/Jameson_Raid" title="Jameson Raid">Jameson Raid</a>. German officials in Berlin had managed to stop the Kaiser from proposing a German protectorate over the Transvaal. The telegram backfired, as the British began to see Germany as a major threat. The British moved their forces from Egypt south into Sudan in 1898, securing complete control of that troublesome region. However, a strong British force unexpectedly confronted a small French military expedition at Fashoda. Salisbury <a href="/wiki/Fashoda_Incident" title="Fashoda Incident">quickly resolved the tensions</a>, and systematically moved toward friendlier relations with France.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Boer_War">Second Boer War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Second Boer War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Second Boer War</a></div>
<p>After gold was discovered in the <a href="/wiki/South_African_Republic" title="South African Republic">South African Republic</a> (called Transvaal) in the 1880s, thousands of British men flocked to the gold mines. Transvaal and its sister republic the <a href="/wiki/Orange_Free_State" title="Orange Free State">Orange Free State</a> were small, rural, independent nations founded by <a href="/wiki/Afrikaners" title="Afrikaners">Afrikaners</a>, who descended from Dutch immigrants to the area before 1800. The newly arrived miners were needed for their labour and business operations but were distrusted by the Afrikaners, who called them "<a href="/wiki/Uitlander" title="Uitlander">uitlanders</a>". The uitlanders heavily outnumbered the Boers in cities and mining districts; they had to pay heavy taxes, and had limited civil rights and no right to vote. The British, jealous of the gold and diamond mines and highly protective of its people, demanded reforms, which were rejected. A small-scale private British effort to overthrow Transvaal's President Paul Kruger, the <a href="/wiki/Jameson_Raid" title="Jameson Raid">Jameson Raid</a> of 1895, was a fiasco and presaged full-scale conflict as all diplomatic efforts failed.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>War started on 11 October 1899 and ended on 31 May 1902 as Great Britain faced the two small far-away Boer nations. The Prime Minister let his extremely energetic colonial minister <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Chamberlain" title="Joseph Chamberlain">Joseph Chamberlain</a> take charge of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> British efforts were based from its Cape Colony and the Colony of Natal. There were some native African allies, but generally both sides avoided using black soldiers. The British war effort was further supported by volunteers from across the Empire. All other nations were neutral, but public opinion in them was largely hostile to Britain. Inside Britain and its Empire there also was a significant <a href="/wiki/Opposition_to_the_Second_Boer_War" title="Opposition to the Second Boer War">opposition to the Second Boer War</a> because of the atrocities and military failures.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British were overconfident and underprepared. Chamberlain and other top London officials ignored the repeated warnings of military advisors that the Boers were well prepared, well armed, and fighting for their homes in a very difficult terrain. The Boers with about 33,000 soldiers, against 13,000 front-line British troops, struck first, besieging Ladysmith, Kimberly, and Mafeking, and winning important battles at Colenso, Magersfontein and Stormberg in late 1899. Staggered, the British fought back, relieved its besieged cities, and prepared to invade first the Orange Free State, and then Transvaal in late 1900. The Boers refused to surrender or negotiate, and reverted to guerrilla warfare. After two years of hard fighting, Britain, using over 400,000 soldiers systematically destroyed the resistance, raising worldwide complaints about brutality. The Boers were fighting for their homes and families, who provided them with food and hiding places. The British solution was to forcefully relocate all the Boer civilians into heavily guarded concentration camps, where 28,000 died of disease. Then it systematically blocked off and tracked down the highly mobile Boer combat units. The battles were small operations; most of the 22,000 British dead were victims of disease. The war cost £217 million and demonstrated the Army urgently needed reforms but it ended in victory for the British and the Conservatives won <a href="/wiki/1900_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1900 United Kingdom general election">the Khaki election of 1900.</a> The Boers were given generous terms, and both former republics were incorporated into the <a href="/wiki/Union_of_South_Africa" title="Union of South Africa">Union of South Africa</a> in 1910.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The war had many vehement critics, predominantly in the Liberal party.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> However, on the whole, the war was well received by the British public, which staged numerous public demonstrations and parades of support.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Soon there were memorials built across Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Strong public demand for news coverage meant that the war was well covered by journalists – including young <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> – and photographers, as well as letter-writers and poets. General <a href="/wiki/Redvers_Buller" title="Redvers Buller">Sir Redvers Buller</a> imposed strict censorship and had no friends in the media, who wrote him up as a blundering buffoon. In dramatic contrast, <a href="/wiki/Frederick_Roberts,_1st_Earl_Roberts" title="Frederick Roberts, 1st Earl Roberts">Field Marshal Frederick Roberts</a> pampered the press, which responded by making him a national hero.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="German_naval_issues">German naval issues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: German naval issues">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1897 Admiral <a href="/wiki/Alfred_von_Tirpitz" title="Alfred von Tirpitz">Alfred von Tirpitz</a> became German Naval Secretary of State and began the transformation of the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_German_Navy" title="Imperial German Navy">Imperial German Navy</a> from a small, coastal defence force to a fleet meant to challenge British naval power. Tirpitz called for a <i>Risikoflotte</i> or "risk fleet" that would make it too risky for Britain to take on <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">Germany</a> as part of wider bid to alter the international balance of power decisively in Germany's favour.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> At the same time German foreign minister <a href="/wiki/Bernhard_von_B%C3%BClow" title="Bernhard von Bülow">Bernhard von Bülow</a> called for <i><a href="/wiki/Weltpolitik" title="Weltpolitik">Weltpolitik</a></i> (world politics). It was the new policy of Germany to assert its claim to be a global power. Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Otto_von_Bismarck" title="Otto von Bismarck">Otto von Bismarck</a>'s policy of <i><a href="/wiki/Realpolitik" title="Realpolitik">Realpolitik</a></i> (realistic politics) was abandoned as Germany was intent on challenging and upsetting international order. The long-run result was the inability of Britain and Germany to be friends or to form an alliance.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Britain reacted to Germany's accelerated naval arms race by major innovations, especially those developed by <a href="/wiki/John_Fisher,_1st_Baron_Fisher" title="John Fisher, 1st Baron Fisher">Admiral Fisher</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> The most important development was unveiled – after Salisbury's death – the entry of <a href="/wiki/HMS_Dreadnought_(1906)" title="HMS Dreadnought (1906)">HMS&#160;<i>Dreadnought</i></a> into service in 1906, which rendered all the world's battleships obsolete and set back German plans.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>
Historians agree that Salisbury was a strong and effective leader in foreign affairs. He had a superb grasp of the issues, and was never a "<a href="/wiki/Splendid_isolation" title="Splendid isolation">splendid isolationist</a>" but rather, says Nancy W. Ellenberger, was:<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"></p><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>A patient, pragmatic practitioner, with a keen understanding of Britain's historic interests&#160;... He oversaw the partition of Africa, the emergence of Germany and the United States as imperial powers, and the transfer of British attention from the Dardanelles to Suez without provoking a serious confrontation of the great powers.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_policy">Domestic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Domestic policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>At home he sought to "kill Home Rule with kindness" by launching a land reform programme which helped hundreds of thousands of Irish peasants gain land ownership and largely ended complaints against English landlords.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The Elementary School Teachers (Superannuation) Act of 1898 enabled teachers to secure an annuity via the payment of voluntary contributions.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> The Elementary Education (Defective and Epileptic Children) Act of 1899 permitted school boards to provide for the education of mentally and physically defective and epileptic children.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours_and_retirement">Honours and retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Honours and retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1895 and 1900 he was honoured with appointments as <a href="/wiki/Lord_Warden_of_the_Cinque_Ports" title="Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports">Lord Warden of the Cinque Ports</a> and <a href="/wiki/High_Steward_of_Westminster_Abbey" title="High Steward of Westminster Abbey">High Steward of the City and Liberty of Westminster</a>, which he held for life.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On 11 July 1902, in failing health and broken hearted over the death of his wife, Salisbury resigned. He was succeeded by his nephew, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a>. King <a href="/wiki/Edward_VII" title="Edward VII">Edward VII</a> conferred upon him the Grand Cross of the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Victorian_Order" title="Royal Victorian Order">Royal Victorian Order</a> (GCVO), with the order star set in brilliants, during his resignation audience.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Last_year:_1902.E2.80.931903"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_year:_1902–1903">Last year: 1902–1903</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Last year: 1902–1903">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Due to breathing difficulties caused by his great weight, Salisbury took to sleeping in a chair at Hatfield House. He also experienced a heart condition and later blood poisoning caused by an ulcerated leg. His death in August 1903 followed a fall from that chair.<sup id="cite_ref-ODNB_8-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ODNB-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Salisbury was buried at <a href="/wiki/St_Etheldreda%27s_Church,_Hatfield" title="St Etheldreda&#39;s Church, Hatfield">St Etheldreda's Church, Hatfield</a>, where his predecessor as prime minister, <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">Lord Melbourne</a>, is also interred.  Salisbury is commemorated with a monumental <a href="/wiki/Cenotaph" title="Cenotaph">cenotaph</a> near the west door of <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>.
</p><p>When Salisbury died his estate was valued at £310,336,<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> (equivalent to £35,453,159 in 2021).<sup id="cite_ref-inflation-UK_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-inflation-UK-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:St_Etheldreda,_Hatfield,_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/220px-St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/330px-St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg/440px-St_Etheldreda%2C_Hatfield%2C_Herts_-_Salisbury_Chapel_-_tomb_of_the_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3264" data-file-height="2448" /></a><figcaption>Monument commemorating Salisbury's burial at St Etheldreda Church, <a href="/wiki/Hatfield,_Hertfordshire" title="Hatfield, Hertfordshire">Hatfield, Hertfordshire</a></figcaption></figure> 
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg/220px-Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg/330px-Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg/440px-Hatfield_GascoyneCecil_statue.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3072" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Statue_of_Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil" class="mw-redirect" title="Statue of Robert Gascoyne-Cecil">Statue of Salisbury</a> in front of the park gates of <a href="/wiki/Hatfield_House" title="Hatfield House">Hatfield House</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Many historians portray Salisbury as a principled statesman of traditional, aristocratic conservatism: a prime minister who promoted cautious imperialism and resisted sweeping parliamentary and franchise reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Robert_Blake,_Baron_Blake" title="Robert Blake, Baron Blake">Robert Blake</a> considers Salisbury "a great foreign minister, [but] essentially negative, indeed reactionary in home affairs".<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Professor P.T. Marsh's estimate is more favourable than Blake's; he portrays Salisbury as a leader who "held back the popular tide for twenty years."<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Professor Paul Smith argues that, "into the 'progressive' strain of modern Conservatism he simply will not fit."<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Colin_Matthew" title="Colin Matthew">H.C.G. Matthew</a> points to "the narrow cynicism of Salisbury."<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> One admirer, conservative historian <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Cowling" title="Maurice Cowling">Maurice Cowling</a>, largely agrees with the critics and says Salisbury found the democracy born of the 1867 and 1884 Reform Acts as "perhaps less objectionable than he had expected—succeeding, through his public persona, in mitigating some part of its nastiness."<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>  Historian Peter T. Marsh states: "In the field of foreign affairs, where he was happiest and most successful, he kept his own counsel and eschewed broad principles of conduct, preferring close-eyed realism and reliability of conduct."<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<ul><li><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/15px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png" decoding="async" width="15" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/22px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg/30px-DodgerBlue_flag_waving.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="249" data-file-height="268" /></span></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Conservatism" title="Portal:Conservatism">Conservatism&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:A_coloured_voting_box.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/16px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/24px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/32px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Politics" title="Portal:Politics">Politics&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Conservatism_sidebar" title="Template:Conservatism sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Conservatism_sidebar" title="Template talk:Conservatism sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Conservatism_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Considerable attention has been devoted to his writings and ideas. The Conservative historian <a href="/wiki/Robert_Blake,_Baron_Blake" title="Robert Blake, Baron Blake">Robert Blake</a> considered Salisbury "the most formidable intellectual figure that the Conservative party has ever produced".<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> In 1977 the Salisbury Group was founded, chaired by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_6th_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 6th Marquess of Salisbury">Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 6th Marquess of Salisbury</a> and named after the 3rd Marquess. It published pamphlets advocating conservative policies.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> The academic quarterly <i><a href="/wiki/The_Salisbury_Review" title="The Salisbury Review">The Salisbury Review</a></i> was named in his honour (by <a href="/wiki/Michael_Oakeshott" title="Michael Oakeshott">Michael Oakeshott</a>) upon its founding in 1982.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Cowling claimed that "The giant of conservative doctrine is Salisbury".<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> It was on Cowling's suggestion that Paul Smith edited a collection of Salisbury's articles from the <i>Quarterly Review</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jones_(historian)" title="Andrew Jones (historian)">Andrew Jones</a> and <a href="/wiki/Michael_Bentley_(historian)" title="Michael Bentley (historian)">Michael Bentley</a> wrote in 1978 that "historical inattention" to Salisbury "involves wilful dismissal of a Conservative tradition which recognizes that threat to humanity when ruling authorities engage in democratic flattery and the threat to liberty in a competitive rush of legislation".<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1967, <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a> (Labour Party prime minister, 1945–51) was asked who he thought was the best prime minister of his lifetime. Attlee immediately replied: "Salisbury".<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 836">&#58;&#8202;836&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_6th_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="Robert Gascoyne-Cecil, 6th Marquess of Salisbury">6th Marquess of Salisbury</a> commissioned <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Roberts_(historian)" class="mw-redirect" title="Andrew Roberts (historian)">Andrew Roberts</a> to write Salisbury's authorised biography, which was published in 1999.
</p><p>After the <a href="/wiki/Bering_Sea_Arbitration" title="Bering Sea Arbitration">Bering Sea Arbitration</a>, Canadian Prime Minister Sir <a href="/wiki/John_Sparrow_David_Thompson" title="John Sparrow David Thompson">John Sparrow David Thompson</a> said of Lord Salisbury's acceptance of the Arbitration Treaty that it was "one of the worst acts of what I regard as a very stupid and worthless life".<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British phrase '<a href="/wiki/Bob%27s_your_uncle" title="Bob&#39;s your uncle">Bob's your uncle</a>' is thought to have derived from Robert Cecil's appointment of his nephew, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Balfour" title="Arthur Balfour">Arthur Balfour</a>, as Chief Secretary for Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-Trahair_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Trahair-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Harare" title="Harare">Fort Salisbury</a> (now Harare) was named in honour of him when it was founded in September 1890. Subsequently, simply known as Salisbury, the city became the capital of <a href="/wiki/Southern_Rhodesia" title="Southern Rhodesia">Southern Rhodesia</a>, from 1890, the <a href="/wiki/Federation_of_Rhodesia_and_Nyasaland" title="Federation of Rhodesia and Nyasaland">Federation of Rhodesia and Nyasaland</a> from 1953 to 1963, <a href="/wiki/Rhodesia" title="Rhodesia">Rhodesia</a> from 1963 to 1979, <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe_Rhodesia" title="Zimbabwe Rhodesia">Zimbabwe Rhodesia</a>, in 1979, and finally <a href="/wiki/Zimbabwe" title="Zimbabwe">Zimbabwe</a>, from 1980. The name was changed to Harare in April 1982, on the second anniversary of Zimbabwe's independence. Cecil Square, near to Parliament, was also named after him and not, as is erroneously but popularly thought, after <a href="/wiki/Cecil_Rhodes" title="Cecil Rhodes">Cecil Rhodes</a>. Other Rhodesian/Zimbabwean connections include the suburbs of Hatfield, Cranborne and New Sarum.
</p><p>To date he is the only British prime minister to sport a full beard. At 6&#160;feet 4&#160;inches (193&#160;cm) tall, he was also the tallest prime minister.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_and_personal_life">Family and personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Family and personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Lord Salisbury was the third son of <a href="/wiki/James_Gascoyne-Cecil,_2nd_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="James Gascoyne-Cecil, 2nd Marquess of Salisbury">James Gascoyne-Cecil, 2nd Marquess of Salisbury</a>, a minor <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative</a> politician. In 1857, he defied his father, who wanted him to marry a rich heiress to protect the family's lands. He instead married <a href="/wiki/Georgina_Gascoyne-Cecil,_Marchioness_of_Salisbury" title="Georgina Gascoyne-Cecil, Marchioness of Salisbury">Georgina Alderson</a>, the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Alderson_(judge)" title="Edward Alderson (judge)">Sir Edward Alderson</a>, a moderately notable judge and of lower social standing than the Cecils. The marriage proved a happy one. Robert and Georgina had eight children, all but one of whom survived infancy. He was an indulgent father and made sure his children had a much better childhood than the one through which he suffered. Cut off from his family money, Robert supported his family through journalism and was later reconciled with his father.<sup id="cite_ref-Roberts2000_7-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Roberts2000-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 30–33, 75, 105–8">&#58;&#8202;30–33,&#8202;75,&#8202;105–8&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Maud_Palmer,_Countess_of_Selborne" title="Maud Palmer, Countess of Selborne">Lady Beatrix Maud Cecil</a> (11 April 1858&#160;&#8211;&#32;27 April 1950); she married <a href="/wiki/William_Palmer,_2nd_Earl_of_Selborne" title="William Palmer, 2nd Earl of Selborne">William Palmer, 2nd Earl of Selborne</a> on 27 October 1883. They had four children.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lady_Gwendolen_Gascoyne-Cecil" class="mw-redirect" title="Lady Gwendolen Gascoyne-Cecil">Lady Gwendolen Cecil</a> (28 July 1860&#160;&#8211;&#32;28 September 1945), author, and biographer of her father; she never married. <a href="/wiki/SS_Gwendolen" title="SS Gwendolen">SS&#160;<i>Gwendolen</i></a>, launched in 1899 on <a href="/wiki/Lake_Nyasa" class="mw-redirect" title="Lake Nyasa">Lake Nyasa</a>, was named after her.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/James_Gascoyne-Cecil,_4th_Marquess_of_Salisbury" title="James Gascoyne-Cecil, 4th Marquess of Salisbury">James Edward Hubert Gascoyne-Cecil, 4th Marquess of Salisbury</a> (23 October 1861&#160;&#8211;&#32;4 April 1947); he married Lady Cicely Gore on 17 May 1887. They had seven children.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lord_William_Cecil_(bishop)" title="Lord William Cecil (bishop)">Lord Rupert Ernest William Cecil, Lord Bishop of Exeter</a> (9 March 1863&#160;&#8211;&#32;23 June 1936); he married Lady Florence Bootle-Wilbraham on 16 August 1887.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Robert_Cecil,_1st_Viscount_Cecil_of_Chelwood" title="Robert Cecil, 1st Viscount Cecil of Chelwood">Lord Edgar Algernon Robert Cecil, 1st Viscount Cecil of Chelwood</a> (14 September 1864&#160;&#8211;&#32;24 November 1958); he married Lady Eleanor Lambton on 22 January 1889.</li>
<li>Hon. Fanny Georgina Mildred Cecil (1865&#160;&#8211;&#32;24 April 1867)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lord_Edward_Cecil" title="Lord Edward Cecil">Lord Edward Herbert Cecil</a> (12 July 1867&#160;&#8211;&#32;13 December 1918); he married Violet Maxse on 18 June 1894. They had two children.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Hugh_Cecil,_1st_Baron_Quickswood" title="Hugh Cecil, 1st Baron Quickswood">Lord Hugh Richard Heathcote Cecil, 1st Baron Quickswood</a> (14 October 1869&#160;&#8211;&#32;10 December 1956)</li></ul>
<p>Salisbury had <a href="/wiki/Prosopagnosia" title="Prosopagnosia">prosopagnosia</a>, a cognitive disorder which makes it difficult to recognize familiar faces.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cabinets_of_Lord_Salisbury">Cabinets of Lord Salisbury</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Cabinets of Lord Salisbury">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="1885.E2.80.931886"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1885–1886">1885–1886</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Robert_Gascoyne-Cecil,_3rd_Marquess_of_Salisbury&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: 1885–1886">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable relarticle mainarticle selfreference noprint">This section is <a href="/wiki/Help:Transclusion" title="Help:Transclusion">transcluded</a> from <a href="/wiki/First_Salisbury_ministry" title="First Salisbury ministry">First Salisbury ministry</a>. <span class="plainlinks" style="font-style: normal; font-size:85%;">(<a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=First_Salisbury_ministry&amp;action=edit">edit</a> &#124; <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=First_Salisbury_ministry&amp;action=history">history</a>)</span></div>
